trade. What began as desperation evolved into opportunity, and soon, the
galaxy turned its eyes toward this haven of sin and indulgence. In the
centuries following its founding, New Xerxes became synonymous with
decadence, vice, and unrivaled ambition. Its districts, known as Sin
Sectors, expanded into neighboring systems, transforming neglected and
lawless worlds into hubs of entertainment and trade. While corporations
and governments elsewhere crumbled under internal strife, New Xerxes
thrived by embracing chaos and commodifying pleasure. The city-state's
Silent Law Charter ensured that no corporation could ever claim
ownership of New Xerxes, shielding it from external manipulation.
However, this independence came at a price: constant political intrigue,
organized crime, and corruption were ingrained into the city's very
identity. Leaders like Elara Voss and Jax Thorne rose to power, each
weaving a balance of governance, crime, and pragmatism to keep the city
afloat. Throughout the Galactic Age, New Xerxes remained wary of foreign
influence. Its black-market bazaars attracted buyers and sellers from
every corner of the galaxy, while its casinos and pleasure houses made
it a magnet for the powerful and desperate alike. Yet even amidst its
wealth, the city was no stranger to tragedy. Wars over trade routes,
failed heists, and cult uprisings threatened its stability, but New
Xerxes endured. Founding to 1851 Year 850 - The Fall of Old Xerxes
During the Myr Cluster Wars, the city of Xerxes---unaffiliated with the
conflict---falls to a catastrophic flooding event caused by an empowered
Water Oni. As waters rise and cities drown, the Board Members of Calix
Corp organize a desperate evacuation aboard the Nimbus Ark. Dome 2
becomes the new home of the survivors, dubbed New Xerxes, carrying the
legacy of their lost city. Year 851 - The Detachment of New Xerxes

Tensions aboard the Nimbus Ark escalate as Dome 2's leadership, led by
Corbin David, clashes with Calix Corp's Board Members and the other
domes over autonomy, resource allocation, and cultural differences.
Under David's leadership, Dome 2 is detached and lands on a nearby
uninhabited planet. This marks the birth of the independent city-state
of New Xerxes. Year 870 - The Founding of the First Casinos With limited
natural resources and surrounded by more dominant powers, New Xerxes
pivots to economic survival through entertainment and vice. Casinos,
pleasure houses, and black-market bazaars are established, drawing
thrill-seekers, gamblers, and opportunists from across the galaxy.
Corbin David declares, "Excess is our export." Year 920 - The Sin
Sectors Expand New Xerxes expands beyond its planetary home, annexing
neighboring systems and transforming them into Sin Sectors, zones of
entertainment, trade, and debauchery. Many systems, neglected by their
original rulers, willingly submit to New Xerxes' influence, exchanging
autonomy for profit. Year 1000 - The Silent Law Charter To prevent
corporate overreach and exploitation, the Silent Law Charter is passed.
Foreign corporations are banned from significant investment, and strict
regulations ensure New Xerxes remains sovereign. This law shields the
city-state from external manipulation but draws ire from powerful
galactic corporations. Year 1100 - The Shadow Coin Crisis A massive
influx of counterfeit currency, known as Shadow Coins, destabilizes New
Xerxes' economy. It is later revealed that the coins originated from
underground factions within the Concrete Cartel. The Mayor at the time,
Elara Voss, strikes a controversial deal with the Cartel, allowing them
limited trade rights in exchange for peace. Year 1310 - The Black Lotus
Incident A cult-like group known as the Black Lotus Syndicate attempts a
hostile takeover of New Xerxes, claiming to offer "true enlightenment
through excess." A bloody campaign unfolds across the city-state's
pleasure hubs and districts, ending with the syndicate's leader executed
in public. This event reinforces New Xerxes' status as a hub of
organized crime. Year 1530 - Election of the Rogue Mayor In a shocking
turn, a well-known black market kingpin, Jax Thorne, is elected Mayor.
Thorne's administration embraces the black market as part of the city's
culture, codifying certain illicit trade practices into law. He famously
declares, "If we profit from sin, let us sin well." Year 1770 - Joining
the Galactic League of States Despite widespread distrust from other
member states, New Xerxes becomes a notable member of the Galactic
League of States. Its role as a neutral hub for trade and diplomacy
ensures its acceptance, though whispers persist about backroom dealings
and favors exchanged for votes. Year 1820 - The Silver Gate Heist One of
the largest heists in galactic history occurs when a rogue faction
infiltrates and steals a massive treasury vault from a New Xerxes moon
colony. The event, celebrated as a "victory of ingenuity," sparks both
outrage and admiration across the galaxy. In 1851 By 1851, New Xerxes
stands as the crown jewel of debauchery and indulgence in the galaxy.
While its size is modest compared to sprawling empires, its influence
far exceeds its borders. Its two sectors, dotted with pleasure

hubs, black-market trade centers, and luxurious entertainment districts,
hum with activity day and night. The Sin Sectors are now legendary
across the galaxy, hosting high-stakes gambling tournaments, illicit
auctions, and trade markets where anything can be bought for the right
price. Its leadership, currently under Mayor Liora Talyn, navigates the
treacherous waters of politics with remarkable skill, balancing the
needs of the people, the demands of organized crime, and the suspicions
of the Galactic League of States. However, cracks are beginning to show
in the city's gilded façade. The Silver Gate Heist of 1820 remains a
dark stain on its security reputation, and whispers of growing internal
corruption threaten to destabilize its fragile balance. Meanwhile, the
city's refusal to respect foreign laws and its harboring of black-market
factions have drawn ire from neighboring nations, including the
ever-watchful Carrigore Space. Still, New Xerxes thrives. It remains a
place where the desperate can find fortune, the powerful can indulge
their darkest desires, and the bold can carve out their legends. As
Mayor Talyn once said, "In New Xerxes, everyone's luck runs out
eventually. The question is: how will you spend it?" Nikios Khanate
Nation in early 3E In the early Third Era, the Centaur of the Nikios
Khanate were scattered tribes roaming the Titanworld's plains, fighting
endless battles against other species and among themselves. Their
society was defined by martial tradition and clan loyalty, with herds
competing for dominance under the leadership of chieftains. The Stampede
Exodus, spurred by the Titanworld's escalating wars, forced them to
adapt quickly, stealing and salvaging technology to escape to the stars.
This tumultuous period laid the foundation for the Khanate's reliance on
strength and mercenary tradition. Nation in 851 By 851 AG, the Nikios
Khanate had established itself as one of the three major powers in
Savage Space. Known for their fierce warriors and organized mercenary
bands, the Khanate wielded significant influence despite their
relatively primitive technology. Their economy relied heavily on tribute
payments extracted from weaker nations and the profits of their
mercenary companies, which were deployed across the galaxy. However,
internal divisions between clans and the strain of outdated technology
hindered their growth. Despite these challenges, their reputation for
disciplined combat and loyalty to contracts kept them relevant in the
volatile Savage Space region. From 851 to 1851 200 - The Stampede Exodus
Like other nations, the Centaur of the Nikios Khanate flee the
Titanworld after devastating wars ravage the continents. Their exodus,
known as the Stampede Exodus, sees entire herds of Centaur migrating to
the stars aboard hastily constructed vessels, often commandeered from
other species through brute force. 450 - The First Tributes Once
established in Savage Space, the Nikios Khanate begins asserting
dominance over weaker factions, demanding tribute in exchange for
protection. The First Tributes solidify their reputation as raiders and
enforcers, with the Khanate employing mercenaries to expand their
influence. 600 - The Mercenary Council The Khan's family formalizes
their control over mercenary operations by founding the Mercenary
Council, a governing body that licenses and coordinates mercenary bands
across the galaxy. This allows the Khanate to

monopolize Centaur-led mercenary work and increase their wealth through
galactic contracts. 800 - The War of Broken Hooves Civil strife breaks
out as factions within the Khanate, led by rival Centaur clans,
challenge the authority of the ruling Khan. The War of Broken Hooves
weakens the nation significantly, forcing the Khanate to rely heavily on
tribute payments and mercenary profits to recover. The conflict ends
with the consolidation of power under the Nikios Khan, but distrust
lingers. 1020 - The Iron Tribute Wars Seeking to expand their influence,
the Khanate launches the Iron Tribute Wars, a series of campaigns
against nearby Savage Space nations to extract additional tribute. While
initially successful, the wars strain their resources and lead to
retaliatory raids from the Azezz Gnollpacks and Ikzo. 1200 - The Scarlet
Divide The Scarlet Divide, a rift between traditionalist and progressive
factions within the Khanate, emerges. Traditionalists favor maintaining
their raiding and tribute-based economy, while progressives push for
greater investment in technology and infrastructure. The divide prevents
the Khanate from advancing technologically, leaving them dependent on
outdated methods. 1400 - The Great Stall The Khanate suffers a period of
stagnation known as the Great Stall, where lack of technological
advancement and internal divisions leave them vulnerable to external
threats. Tribute payments dwindle as their ability to enforce them
weakens, forcing the Khanate to lean heavily on their mercenary
reputation to survive. 1600 - The Rise of the Silver Khan A charismatic
leader known as the Silver Khan unites the Mercenary Council under his
rule, revitalizing the Khanate's influence. His reforms centralize power
and increase mercenary efficiency, allowing the Khanate to reclaim lost
territories and demand tribute with renewed strength. 1750 - The Writ of
Mercenary Sovereignty The Khanate drafts the Writ of Mercenary
Sovereignty, a set of laws and traditions formalizing the Khanate's role
as the galaxy's premier mercenary nation. This attracts contracts from
major powers across the galaxy, revitalizing their economy and
consolidating their status in Savage Space. 1851 - The Era of Golden
Stirrups By 1851, the Nikios Khanate has stabilized under the legacy of
the Silver Khan's reforms. While still lagging technologically compared
to other nations, their unmatched mercenary tradition and mastery of
tribute collection secure their place in Savage Space. The Khanate
thrives as a semi-civilized but influential power, blending ancient
customs with pragmatic diplomacy and warfare. In 1851 By 1851 AG, the
Nikios Khanate had entered a golden age known as the Era of Golden
Stirrups, revitalized by the reforms of the Silver Khan. Their
centralized governance and efficient mercenary operations made them
indispensable to galactic conflicts, while their ability to enforce
tribute ensured a steady flow of resources. Though their technological
progress remained slow, their mastery of warfare and tribute collection
allowed them to maintain dominance in Savage Space. The Khanate, with
its deep-rooted traditions and pragmatic diplomacy, stood as a symbol of
resilience and martial excellence, a force respected and feared across
the galaxy. Orog Clans of Xonoth

Nation in early 3E In the early Third Era, the Orogs of Xonoth were a
savage and fractured people, raiding and pillaging their way to
dominance on the Titanworld. Their brutal campaigns earned them the
resources and knowledge needed to escape the planet during the Iron
Exodus, cementing their reputation as fierce and irreligious conquerors.
These early years were marked by constant infighting and raiding, as the
clans struggled to adapt to their new existence among the stars. Nation
in 851 By 851 AG, the Orog Clans had solidified their control over
Xonoth, their loosely organized but cohesive alliance governed by the
Pact of Iron Chains. Their economy thrived on the spoils of piracy,
raiding, and mercenary work, while their slaves fueled their industries
and bloodcraft technologies. Despite their weaker naval capabilities,
their prowess in ground combat and their willingness to operate outside
galactic norms made them a feared and infamous power. From 851 to 1851
Prior to 851, in 500 - The Iron Exodus The Orogs pillage their way off
the Titanworld during the Iron Exodus, acquiring stolen starships and
salvaged technology. The clans carve a brutal path across the galaxy,
establishing themselves in the region now known as Xonoth, a cluster of
resource-rich but contested systems. 900 - The Rise of Tyroke the God
Emperor Tyroke, the legendary Orog leader, departs from the Titanworld,
rejecting his empire to pursue a mercenary life among the stars. His
reputation as a champion of Yu and Core earns him a following of both
Orogs and opportunistic adventurers. Tyroke establishes the Xonoth
Mercenary Guild, accepting payments in souls, magical relics, and
worship rather than conventional wealth. \_910 - The Chains of Blood
Rebellion Orc slaves, often sold to the Orogs by the Shatter Stance,
rise up in revolt on an industrialized Orog world. The rebellion is
crushed, but the clans adopt harsh new traditions that enslave entire
populations to fuel their war machine. This results in the introduction
of 'Blood Chains,' a set of brutal rites of conquest binding vassals
into eternal servitude. 1000 - The Pact of Iron Chains The Orog clans
formalize their governance through the Pact of Iron Chains, a loose
alliance that unites the clans under a council of warlords. Each warlord
rules their territory with autonomy but must send tribute and warriors
to Xonoth's central council during times of war. This pact allows the
clans to maintain cohesion while preserving their brutal independence.
1100 - The Blackenhold Uprising A slave rebellion breaks out on
Blackenhold, one of the Orog clans' largest strongholds. Led by an orc
chieftain named Zarok One-Eye, the rebellion is crushed after a
prolonged siege. In response, the Orogs implement stricter controls over
their enslaved populations, intensifying their reputation as brutal
overlords. \_1235 - The Conquest of Tarrasj's Wound The Orogs invade and
claim the volcanic world Tarrasj's Wound, known for its molten
landscapes and infernal creatures. They forge entire cities into its
chasms, drawing power from its core to enhance their weapons. Rumors

claim this awakens an ancient being bound deep below. The rumors spur
weapons development, resulting in a new ship weapon - Inferno Spears,
weapons capable of splitting warships in half with molten energy. 1250 -
The Forge of Bloodcraft Xonoth's engineers develop Bloodcrafting, a
unique fusion of magical and mechanical technology that binds the life
force of slaves to fuel ships and weapons. While incredibly effective,
this practice draws the ire of other Galactic League members, further
isolating the Orogs diplomatically. 1400 - The Shattered Accord The
Orogs clash with neighboring factions in the Shattered Accord War, a
series of territorial disputes that highlight their naval weaknesses.
While victorious on the ground, the clans lose control of several key
systems due to their inability to counter enemy fleets effectively.
\_1501 - The Corebrand Rebellion A faction of Orog warlords rejects the
clan's mercenary traditions, instead offering fealty to Core's flame.
The Orogs, backed by the holy order known as the Corebrand Warband,
challenges the High Warlord's rule, causing an internal civil war that
devastates Xonoth's economy. They begrudgingly form alliances with human
Core nations in the galaxy, but eventually migrate from the region as
nations of their faith are put through militaristic trials. 1600 - The
Chains of Command Reforms To counter their naval deficiencies, the Orog
clans invest heavily in advanced ship designs and mercenary fleets. The
Chains of Command Reforms establish a centralized naval command,
combining technological innovation with their formidable ground forces
to create a more balanced military. 1700 - The War of Karak Bakhmut The
Orog clans profit from the War of Karak Bakhmut by offering their
mercenaries to both sides in exchange for magical items and the promise
of conquered territory. Their opportunistic involvement earns them
disdain but bolsters their economy and technological base. 1800 - The
Chainfire Covenant The Orog clans negotiate the Chainfire Covenant, a
pact with a rogue faction of the Star Tsars to exchange antimagic
technology for mercenary services. This deal strengthens the Orogs'
ability to counteract magicdependent foes but further tarnishes their
reputation within the Galactic League. 1851 - A Brutal Balance By 1851,
the Orog Clans of Xonoth remain a powerful but isolated nation. Their
ground forces and mercenary reputation are unmatched, but their naval
presence, while improved, still limits their ability to project power.
Their reliance on slavery and their rejection of galactic norms make
them a pariah state within the Galactic League, yet their resilience and
adaptability ensure their continued survival. In 1851 By 1851 AG, the
Orog Clans of Xonoth are a nation defined by their contradictions:
fiercely independent yet loosely united, technologically innovative yet
brutal in their reliance on slavery. Their mercenary tradition remains a
cornerstone of their economy, and their improvements in naval strength
have bolstered their standing. However, their defiance of galactic norms
keeps them isolated within the Galactic League of States, a pariah
nation that thrives on conflict and conquest while rejecting the values
of the broader galaxy. Orro Nation in early 3E

In the early Third Era, the Outer Ring emerged from the chaos of the
Titan Exodus as an isolated necromantic state. Orro, the god of death,
guided his worshippers to establish a rigid society ruled by the High
Council of Necromancers. The Council's power was absolute, governing
from paradise worlds hidden within the Outer Ring. The nation's strength
came from its mastery over death, harvesting the bodies of the fallen
and transforming them into undead armies. Orro's reputation grew as its
Bone Worlds---celestial masses infested with endless undead---drifted
ominously near its borders. These grisly artifacts served as both a
deterrent and a statement of Orro's dominance, warning wouldbe invaders
of the nation's horrific capabilities. Nation in 851 By 851, Orro had
become a grim and isolated necrotic powerhouse. Its paradise worlds
flourished under the protection of the High Council, but the outer
colonies bore the brunt of Orro's endless campaigns to maintain
dominance. The Skeletal Vanguard, reformed in 900, patrolled Orro's
borders, ensuring no intruder could breach their sanctuaries. Carrigore
Space's raids tested Orro's defenses but also provided a steady supply
of bodies for its undead armies. While the elite lived in opulence among
the paradise worlds, the outer colonies endured harsh conditions and
nearconstant warfare. Orro's strength lay in its terrifying ability to
weaponize death on a galactic scale, ensuring that even its enemies
feared to challenge it openly. From 851 to 1851 Year 851 - Era of the
Pale Guardian The necromantic High Council of Orro declares the
Pale---Orro's bound demon---a divine emissary. It becomes a key
enforcer, overseeing Orro's borders and ensuring total obedience to the
Council's decrees. Paradise worlds flourish, but the outer territories
experience increased raids by Carrigore Space, testing Orro's defenses.
Year 900 - The Skeletal Vanguard Reforms The Skeletal Vanguard, Orro's
undead legions, is reorganized to include more advanced necromantic
constructs. This innovation allows Orro to repel raids from Carrigore
Space with devastating precision. The reform solidifies Orro's
reputation as a military power capable of wielding death itself as a
weapon. Year 1100 - Rise of the Sepulchral Guilds The Sepulchral Guilds,
necromantic artisans who craft undead monstrosities, gain prominence.
Their creation of new Flesh Titans and Gravitic Necroliths
revolutionizes Orro's offensive capabilities. These constructs are
deployed to maintain Orro's borders, cementing the guilds' political
power within the High Council. Year 1250 - The Veil of Black Moons A
rare celestial alignment disrupts Orro's magical networks, leading to an
uprising in the outer territories. The High Council deploys the Pale to
crush the rebellion. While the rebellion is quelled, dissent lingers,
and Orro fortifies its paradise worlds further, isolating them from the
galaxy. Year 1400 - The Pact of Eternal Lament To counter mounting raids
and economic isolation, Orro enters a grim alliance with a minor
necrotic sect in the Outer Straits. The pact provides resources but
destabilizes Orro's necromantic balance, sparking a series of internal
purges known as the Bone Cleansings. Year 1500 - The Wraithforged
Rebellion

Disillusioned necromancers within Orro's military defect, creating the
Wraithforged Cabal in the outer sectors. These rogue elements attempt to
establish a breakaway necromantic state. The High Council responds with
overwhelming force, but their success comes at the cost of draining
resources from the paradise worlds. Year 1700 - Tendrils of Decay:
Campaign Against the Second Elrossi Imperium Orro launches its infamous
invasion of the Second Elrossi Imperium, sending Tendrils of
Decay---waves of undead across several sectors. Gravitational necrotic
magic drags Bone Worlds into enemy systems, devastating Elrossi
territory. Despite initial successes, resistance stiffens, and Orro's
momentum slows after two decades of grinding war. Still, both living and
undead servants of Orro litter the Elrossi controlled sectors - with the
most holy of Elrossi proclaiming it would be centuries before every
system had truly recovered. Year 1725 - The Ashen Stalemate Orro's
campaign against the Elrossi Imperium stagnates. While several sectors
remain under Orro's control, supply lines falter, and internal dissent
grows. The Pale leads a final offensive but fails to break the Elrossi
stronghold of Elyria's Redoubt. Year 1800 - The Pale's Disappearance The
Pale vanishes mysteriously after returning from the Ashen Stalemate.
With its greatest enforcer gone, Orro's military efforts falter further,
and dissent among the outer necromantic sects resurfaces. Year 1825 -
The Council of Fivefold Shadows In response to growing instability, the
High Council consolidates power, reorganizing into the Council of
Fivefold Shadows. Each member oversees a key aspect of Orro's
governance: necromancy, military, diplomacy, research, and faith. This
structure stabilizes the nation and revitalizes its internal
administration. Year 1850 - Age of Quiet Dominion By 1850, Orro has
withdrawn from its outer campaigns and focuses on fortifying its
paradise worlds. Its influence across the Border Systems remains, but
the nation is seen as a brooding giant---unmoving, enigmatic, and
terrifying. Bone Worlds orbit contested regions, serving as reminders of
Orro's past horrors. In 1851 By 1851, Orro was a shadow of its former
self, yet its name still invoked fear across the galaxy. The Council of
Fivefold Shadows ruled with iron discipline, maintaining order within
the paradise worlds while keeping the outer colonies under tight
control. Bone Worlds still drifted near contested regions, serving as
grim reminders of Orro's past conquests. Though its borders had
contracted, Orro remained an enigmatic and dangerous power. Its Bone
Worlds, Skeletal Vanguard, and necromantic technologies ensured its
survival, while the disappearance of the Pale left its people with a mix
of unease and hope. Orro, the god of death, continued to guide his
followers, promising that the Age of Quiet Dominion would someday give
way to a new era of decay and conquest. P&D Corporate Space Nation in
early 3E In the wake of the Rot-Rising on Harroland, a band of legendary
adventurers formed the Pump and Dump Trade Company in 131. Their daring
exploits, entrepreneurial vision, and ruthless corporatism quickly
secured them prominence. As the Titan Exodus began, the trade company
acquired stolen and salvaged ships, becoming one of the first nations to
escape the Titanworld. Rebranded as Pump & Dump Corporate Space, the
company left its adventurer origins behind, transforming into a colossal
trade conglomerate.

Nation in 851 By 851, P&D Corporate Space stands as a model of ruthless
corporate efficiency and galactic innovation. Its board of directors
rules with an iron grip, overseeing a sprawling network of industrial
planets, mining stations, and research facilities. The corporation
prioritizes profit above all, leveraging advanced robotics, automation,
and bioengineered workers to maximize productivity. While many galactic
powers view P&D as cold and calculating, its technological advancements
make it indispensable. P&D's economy thrives on monopolies in key
sectors such as energy refinement, weapons manufacturing, and cybernetic
augmentation. Its relentless pursuit of market dominance has also earned
it a reputation for aggression, with hostile takeovers extending to both
corporate rivals and independent star systems. The corporation's
military arm, the Titanium Directive, enforces its interests, often
skirting the line between private security and outright warlordism. From
851 to 1851 Year 851 - The Conglomerate Horizon P&D Corporate Space
establishes its first Galactic Logistics Network, connecting trade hubs
across dozens of systems. While their shipping and procurement services
already dominate, this milestone solidifies their role as the backbone
of interstellar commerce. Competitors struggle to match P&D's seamless
delivery, and smaller companies are quickly absorbed or outcompeted.
Year 900 - The Duergar Contract P&D strikes an exclusive agreement to
exploit Duergar labor across the galaxy, branding it as a "Mutual
Opportunity Program." Under this arrangement, Duergar become indentured
workers in massive industrial plants and shipyards, often in deplorable
conditions. Protests erupt across certain systems, but the Chairmen
deflect accusations of exploitation, claiming the program provides
stability and employment. Year 950 - The Silent Expansion While other
nations are reeling from wars and incursions (such as Karim Ibn Al
Daveed's campaigns), P&D quietly expands its influence by offering
"stability packages" to small systems. These packages include economic
aid in exchange for the systems adopting P&D's governance and
infrastructure. Critics call this underhanded colonization, but local
populations often see it as a preferable alternative to chaos. Year
1020 - The Miracle Fleet The Miracle Fleet---a massive convoy of ships
branded as "Salvation Haulers"---rescues millions of refugees from
war-torn regions across the galaxy. P&D receives universal praise for
its humanitarian efforts, but leaked internal memos suggest the
operation was a test run for new automated shipping fleets. This
revelation sparks debates over P&D's true motives and ethics. Year
1100 - The Great Backlash A massive workers' revolt breaks out in a
P&D-operated mining system after harsh conditions and repeated safety
failures lead to a catastrophic collapse. The revolt spreads to multiple
labor hubs, particularly among Duergar and Orog workers. P&D suppresses
the uprisings with overwhelming mercenary force, blaming "union
agitators" for the unrest. The brutality of the suppression tarnishes
P&D's reputation for decades. Year 1200 - The Adventurer Corporate Plan
P&D launches its Adventurer Corporate Plan, a nostalgic nod to its
founding heroes. Guilds and adventurers are offered lucrative contracts
to exclusively use P&D products---ranging from weaponry and survival
tools to ships and automata---in exchange for discounts and sponsorship.
The move is wildly successful, leading to the formation of

Company Champions, P&D-endorsed guilds that act as walking
advertisements. This is the first time in years that P&D has a positive
public reception, but it does not overshadow the corporation's stains.
Year 1300 - The Legacy Lawsuits Over a hundred minor adventurer guilds
file lawsuits against P&D Corporate Space, claiming they were misled by
the Corporate Adventurer Plan---a subscription model exploiting guilds
through overpriced gear and mandatory service fees. The lawsuits are
quickly buried under bureaucracy and legal loopholes, but resentment
grows within the adventuring community. Year 1400 - The Sovranty Threads
A series of inexplicable decisions made by P&D Chairmen cause market
volatility, leading analysts to suspect external manipulation. Rumors
spread that certain Chairmen are puppets of the mysterious Sovranty of
Sin'Dal. However, investigations uncover nothing concrete. The event is
dismissed as a conspiracy theory, though whispers of "The Silent
Boardroom" persist for centuries. Year 1500 - The Virtual Chains Act P&D
Corporate Space introduces Augmented Labor Contracts, binding workers to
virtual agreements that enforce strict productivity quotas via neural
implants. While efficient, the program sparks galaxy-wide protests. The
Virtual Chains controversy leads to limited reforms, but P&D's hold over
its laborers remains largely intact. Year 1730 - Joining the Galactic
League of States P&D is admitted into the Galactic League of States
after decades of lobbying and political maneuvering. While many nations
distrust the corporate conglomerate, its logistical networks and
economic reach are too valuable to exclude. P&D's membership cements its
influence across the galactic political landscape. Year 1790 - Rise of
the Autocorps P&D unveils the Autocorps, an entirely automated workforce
of drones, haulers, and management AIs that eliminate the need for many
human and Duergar workers. Labor unions decry this as economic warfare,
leading to organized sabotage campaigns. P&D retaliates with private
security forces, escalating tensions in systems reliant on manual labor.
Year 1850 - The Boardroom Purge A series of unexplained "resignations"
occur among P&D's upper management. Internal power struggles lead to
rumors of assassinations, coups, and the reshuffling of roles. Publicly,
P&D announces a "restructuring initiative" to ensure stability and
innovation, but the events leave the galactic community uneasy. In 1851
P&D Corporate Space stands as the largest corporate nation in the
galaxy, controlling entire systems as "logistical hubs." Their shipping
empire reaches every corner of the known universe, offering anything
from weapons and food to illegal goods---if the price is right. Though
they are a member of the Galactic League of States, many nations view
them with suspicion, fearing their economic stranglehold. The Board of
Chairmen, shrouded in mystery, continues to rule the nation with an iron
fist. The Autocorps have eliminated most manual labor, leaving Duergar
populations increasingly obsolete and restless. Persistent whispers of
the Sovranty of Sin'Dal's influence create an air of paranoia among the
Chairmen. While P&D Corporate Space remains the galaxy's greatest
purveyor of goods, its exploitative practices and unchecked power leave
a stain upon its legacy. The shadow of its founders---the legendary
heroes who dreamed of a better world---has long since faded into
corporate propaganda. Pillwake

Nation in early 3E In the early Third Era, Dr. Pill---a notorious
kingpin of the Pill Mob---rose from death and reclaimed control of his
vast criminal empire. His resurrection sparked a rapid expansion of
Pillwake's operations, as smugglers and enforcers rallied behind him.
Within decades, Pillwake achieved interstellar travel, using illicit
funds and arcane rituals to fuel its rise as a shadowy power in the
galaxy. Nation in 851 By 851 AG, Pillwake had established itself as a
formidable interstellar criminal syndicate under Dr. Pill's unyielding
leadership. Operating with a 1920s-1930s mafia aesthetic, the Pill Mob
Families controlled smuggling routes, black markets, and illegal
economies across the southern galaxy. Their shadowy influence rivaled
legitimate nations, while Dr. Pill's parasitic immortality ensured his
continued dominance. From 150 to 1851 150 - The Resurrection of Dr. Pill
After centuries trapped in limbo, Dr. Pill's spirit is freed through a
dark ritual, and he manifests a new body. Reclaiming leadership of the
Pill Mob, Dr. Pill unveils his plan to transform Pillwake into a
spacefaring empire. Within a year, massive investments, fueled by
smuggling profits and fervent loyalty to Dr. Pill, result in Pillwake
achieving interstellar travel. 200 - The Shadow Cartel Pillwake
establishes the Shadow Cartel, an interconnected network of smugglers,
assassins, and enforcers. Dr. Pill introduces new substances imbued with
minor magical effects, further addicting populations across rival
systems and expanding his power base. Rival underworld groups begin
falling under Pillwake's sway. 450 - The Black Scrip Revolt A group of
disillusioned Pillwake leaders attempts to overthrow Dr. Pill, angered
by his use of the population's psychic essence for immortality. The
rebellion, known as the Black Scrip Revolt, is crushed in a brutal
campaign. Dr. Pill introduces the Flesh Pledge, a ritual binding his
most loyal followers' souls to him in eternal servitude. The rebellion's
remnants are publicly executed, solidifying his control. 700 - The First
Merchant War Pillwake clashes with the trade syndicates of the Trade
League of Castiel, attempting to undermine their control of galactic
trade routes. The war devolves into shadow operations, with smuggling
rings, hired assassins, and poisoned supply lines wreaking havoc. Though
neither side claims victory, the conflict cements Pillwake as a force to
be reckoned with. 980 - The Pillwake Gambit Pillwake launches a
devastating assault on the Concrete Cartel, targeting their smuggling
routes and critical assets. This ignites a decade-long war in which
Pillwake's advanced ships and ruthless enforcers overwhelm the cartel's
outdated forces. Dr. Pill personally oversees the campaign, marking the
Pill Mob's first major step toward galactic dominance. 1070 - The
Shattered Spire Incident In retaliation for an attempted assassination,
Pillwake obliterates the Shattered Spire, a major Concrete Cartel
orbital hub. The act kills tens of thousands, humiliates the cartel, and
earns Pillwake a reputation for unrelenting brutality. Dr. Pill uses the
destruction as a warning to all rivals: betrayal will not be tolerated.

1200 - The Syndicate Ascendancy Dr. Pill restructures Pillwake into a
formalized criminal syndicate, dividing territories into "Families"
overseen by loyal mob bosses. Each Family manages trade routes,
production facilities, and smuggling operations. Dr. Pill enforces
absolute loyalty, while Families compete for his favor, ensuring
efficiency and innovation across their empire. 1310 - The Red Fang
Accord Splinter groups from the collapsing Concrete Cartel align with
Pillwake, offering resources and territories in exchange for survival.
The Red Fang Accord turns the tides against remaining cartel loyalists,
as Pillwake absorbs key systems. This further destabilizes the cartel
and expands Pillwake's dominion. 1400 - The Concrete Collapse With the
Concrete Cartel reduced to scattered warlords and lawless regions,
Pillwake claims vast portions of their former territory. Dr. Pill
establishes "The Pillwake Trade Spine", a vast network of smuggling
lanes connecting systems across the southern galaxy. Rival nations grow
increasingly wary of Pillwake's unchecked influence. 1600 - The Rise of
the Underblack Pillwake unveils the Underblack, a hidden shadow market
that operates across hundreds of systems, selling illegal goods,
advanced weapons, and magical artifacts to any buyer. The Underblack
becomes an economic powerhouse, generating immense wealth while
undermining lawful economies. 1700 - The Flesh Markets of Xyros A
notorious incident occurs on Xyros, where Pillwake operatives flood a
system with addictive substances, causing a societal collapse. The
"Flesh Markets", grim arenas for gladiatorial combat and black-market
auctions, emerge under Dr. Pill's watch. While other nations condemn the
event, Pillwake reaps profit from the chaos. 1820 - The Eclipse
Syndicate Dr. Pill introduces the Eclipse Syndicate, a covert
organization of elite enforcers and operatives bound to him through his
parasitic rituals. The Eclipse Syndicate expands Pillwake's control
through sabotage, assassination, and infiltration of rival governments.
Rumors spread that Dr. Pill's influence stretches far beyond what even
his allies believe. 1851 - The Ascendant Underworld By 1851, Pillwake
has evolved into the galaxy's most dangerous and influential criminal
empire. Dr. Pill's rule is unchallenged, and his Pill Mob Families
dominate smuggling routes, shadow markets, and illicit economies across
vast regions of space. The Underblack remains a hub of illegal trade,
while the Eclipse Syndicate ensures that rivals are silenced before they
become threats. Though still a criminal syndicate at its core, Pillwake
operates with the efficiency of a galactic power, with Dr. Pill standing
as its eternal ruler---an immortal kingpin whose shadow stretches across
the stars. In 1851 By 1851 AG, Pillwake has become the galaxy's
undisputed criminal empire. Its smuggling networks, Underblack shadow
markets, and elite Eclipse Syndicate operatives ensure dominance across
hundreds of systems. Dr. Pill remains a dark legend, his immortality
secured through parasitic rituals that drain entire populations. While
other nations condemn their actions, Pillwake thrives, evolving into a
shadow state that manipulates galactic economies from the darkness.
Providence

Nation in early 3E In the chaos of the Titan Exodus, a diverse group of
refugees---including humans, elves, dwarves, orcs, halflings, and
more---found themselves stranded on the remnants of a shattered moon
near the Titancore. Initially divided by racial tensions and resource
competition, these survivors faced relentless raids from Hobgoblin
warbands and environmental collapse. Survival became their unifying
force. The Pact of Survival, signed in 150, marked the first step toward
unity, as leaders from each faction agreed to pool resources and defend
one another. Over the next century, these desperate alliances grew into
something more: a collective vision for a nation where no race
dominated, and all shared in governance and prosperity. Though tenuous
at first, this vision became the foundation of Providence, a nation born
of necessity but sustained by hope. Nation in 851 By 851, Providence had
transformed from a struggling collective into a thriving multi-racial
nation. Governed by the Council of Hands, where each major race held
equal representation, Providence embodied a vision of cooperation and
balance. The Sentinels of Balance, a multi-racial peacekeeping force,
patrolled its territories, ensuring disputes were resolved with
diplomacy rather than violence. The nation's unique strength lay in its
diversity. Each race contributed its skills and traditions, creating a
society that thrived on hybrid innovations. Dwarves mastered
engineering; elves advanced magical technology; orcs excelled in
agriculture and natural remedies; humans dominated trade; and halflings
built thriving artisan communities. This synergy powered The Age of
Rediscovery, a period of rapid advancement fueled by relics salvaged
from the Second Material Plane. Providence had also become a key player
in galactic trade, leveraging its neutrality and hybrid artifacts to
mediate disputes and broker alliances. Yet, tensions simmered beneath
the surface. Racial factions vied for influence in the Council, and
territorial disputes occasionally erupted into unrest. Despite these
challenges, Providence stood as a shining example of unity in a
fractured galaxy. From 851 to 1851 Year 851 -- The Founding of Greater
Providence After decades of work, the nation establishes a capital that
rivals any city in the galaxy at this time. The Council of Hands,
composed of representatives from each race, is established to govern.
Their first act is the Charter of Balance, a foundational document
enshrining equality and shared governance among the races of their grand
city and maintained sectors. Year 990 -- The War of the Golden Fields
Providence faces its first existential threat when neighboring warlords
invade its fertile heartlands. Initially fragmented, the Council
struggles to mount a defense, but a charismatic dwarven commander, Tharn
Goldshield, unites the factions. The victory cements the idea that
diversity, when harnessed, can be Providence's greatest strength. Year
1200 -- The Rift of Shadows Tensions between Providence's racial
factions boil over during a territorial dispute involving ancient elven
ruins. The Rift of Shadows, a period of civil unrest and skirmishes,
threatens to fracture the nation. The conflict ends with the creation of
the Sentinels of Balance, a multi-racial peacekeeping force empowered to
mediate disputes and enforce the Charter. Year 1387 -- The Age of
Rediscovery

Providence enters a period of rapid growth and technological advancement
as they begin salvaging and studying relics from the Second Material
Plane. Teams of scholars and adventurers, funded by the Council, uncover
ancient technologies that bolster the nation's economy and defense
capabilities. Their hybrid innovations give Providence a unique edge in
trade and diplomacy. Year 1400 -- The Binding Accord Providence forges
the Binding Accord with neighboring nations, establishing itself as a
neutral trade hub and mediator in galactic disputes. The Accord grants
Providence protection in exchange for their expertise in ancient relics
and a steady supply of salvaged artifacts. Year 1550 -- The Whispering
Rot A mysterious plague known as the Whispering Rot sweeps through
Providence, affecting its magical artifacts and structures. The Council
unites behind an orcish healer, Gralga Rootspeaker, whose knowledge of
ancient cures saves countless lives. The crisis strengthens the bonds
between Providence's factions. Year 1685 -- Joining the Galactic League
of States Providence officially joins the Galactic League of States,
presenting itself as a bastion of harmony and progress. Their delegates
emphasize the importance of cultural diversity and cooperation, earning
them respect as mediators and trade partners. Year 1820 -- The Dawnline
Compact Faced with rising tensions in the galaxy, Providence brokers the
Dawnline Compact, an agreement between member races to provide mutual
defense against external threats. This pact strengthens Providence's
military presence while preserving its emphasis on unity and
cooperation. In 1851 By 1851, Providence had become a respected member
of the Galactic League of States and a beacon of interracial
cooperation. The Dawnline Compact, signed in 1820, had strengthened its
military and solidified its role as a mediator in galactic conflicts.
The nation's hybrid innovations---combining elven magic, dwarven
engineering, and human ingenuity---were highly sought after, driving
economic prosperity. The Sentinels of Balance evolved into an elite
peacekeeping force, quelling unrest within Providence's borders and
serving as emissaries to other nations. However, Providence's harmony
was fragile. Factions within the Council of Hands clashed over resource
allocation and the growing influence of certain racial groups. The
remnants of the Whispering Rot, a plague from the 1300s that corrupted
magical artifacts, occasionally resurfaced, causing localized crises.
The influx of refugees and traders brought new cultural influences but
also destabilized the nation's carefully maintained balance. Externally,
Providence faced pressure from galactic powers eager to exploit its
relic-rich territories. Yet, its commitment to neutrality and the
Dawnline Compact's mutual defense agreement deterred most threats. By
navigating these challenges with pragmatism and diplomacy, Providence
retained its identity as a nation where many races thrived together,
proving that unity amidst diversity could endure in even the most
fractured galaxy. Reclaimer's League Nation in early 3E In the early
Third Era, the Gnomish Technocracy thrived as a beacon of innovation and
ambition. Its obsession with scientific progress led to rapid
advancements in technomagic and cybernetics but alienated neighboring
nations. The Halfling Extermination Campaign of 844 cast a long shadow
over their achievements, fostering distrust and internal dissent. By
851, the Technocracy was a tightly controlled but deeply divided state,
grappling with the moral and political fallout of its past actions.

Nation in 851 By 851, the Gnomish Technocracy had become a nation of
contradictions: a technological powerhouse burdened by its history and
mistrusted by the galaxy. Its automated defenses and self-sustaining
infrastructure made it nearly impenetrable, but its insular policies and
the lingering stigma of the Halfling Genocide left it isolated. The coup
that ousted its leadership marked a turning point, sparking both hope
and uncertainty for its future. From 851 to 1851 Year 851 - The Coup of
the Technocrats Internal dissent culminates in the expulsion of the
Gnomish leadership responsible for the Halfling Genocide of 844.
Technarch Revallim and his closest allies are overthrown, and the
Council of Innovation assumes control. The coup leads to sweeping
reforms but solidifies the Technocracy's isolation in galactic politics.
Year 900 - The Core Purity Doctrine The Technocracy bans all
non-technological magic within its borders, furthering its dedication to
technomagic. This decision alienates traditional arcane practitioners
and fosters an underground resistance, further dividing the nation
culturally and politically. Year 975 - The Mechanized Renaissance A
golden age of innovation begins, marked by the development of advanced
cybernetic augmentation and selfreplicating machinery. These
technologies attract rogue scholars and inventors from across the galaxy
but deepen mistrust from neighboring nations. Year 1020 - The Conflict
of Proxy Systems Blackstar Corporation and the Gnomish Technocracy clash
indirectly over access to rare resources in neutral systems. This
"corporate cold war" escalates tensions, with the Stars of the
Triprophets intervening to mediate an uneasy truce. Year 1200 - The
Great Vault Initiative The Technocracy completes its most ambitious
project: a network of "Great Vaults," subterranean megastructures
housing its population and archives. These vaults are designed to ensure
survival against external threats, but rumors circulate about their
hidden purposes. Year 1376 - The Shatterling Crisis An experiment in
interdimensional travel leads to the catastrophic loss of the colony
Shard-One. Survivors, now called Shatterlings, return decades later with
warped minds and bodies. This incident erodes public trust in the
Council of Innovation. Year 1400 - The Silent Papers Scandal Leaked
documents reveal the Technocracy's clandestine ownership of assets
across the galaxy through shell companies. Public outcry forces the
Council to sever many of these ties, but the revelations permanently
tarnish the Technocracy's reputation. Year 1480 - The Barrier Wars
Neighboring systems launch a coordinated attack on the Technocracy,
citing fears of its unchecked technological advancement. The Gnomes
repel the assault using their automated defense grids but withdraw
further into isolation as a result.

Year 1500 - The Hidden Vault Directive Rumors of a secret project under
the Technocracy's highest council surface. Allegedly focused on
achieving interdimensional ascension or developing a failsafe against
galactic threats, the project becomes a source of galactic speculation.
Year 1700 - The Disappearance of the Gnomish Technocracy Without
warning, the entire Gnomish Technocracy vanishes, leaving behind only
empty systems and automated defenses. Theories about their fate range
from technological ascension to self-destruction, with no definitive
answers. Year 1750 - The Reclaimer's League Forms The Gnomish diaspora
forms the Reclaimer's League, a coalition dedicated to uncovering the
fate of the Technocracy. This group attracts scholars, adventurers, and
opportunists, becoming a significant cultural and political force. The
Reclaimer's League does not get recognized as a nation by the Galactic
League of States, but does get support from across the galaxy almost
universally. Year 1790 - The Rediscovery of Shard-One Reclaimers
discover remnants of the lost Shard-One colony. The site holds
fragmented data pointing to the Technocracy's final experiments but
raises more questions than answers. Year 1820 - The Vault of Echoes
Explorers uncover a functioning Great Vault, its automated systems still
operational. Inside, cryptic messages and dormant machines hint at the
Technocracy's plans but offer no clear answers. Year 1851 - The Legacy
Unfolds By 1851, the Reclaimer's League stands as a vibrant yet
enigmatic entity, balancing the pursuit of answers with its own
political aspirations. The ruins of the Technocracy remain a mix of
marvel and mystery, their secrets tantalizing yet elusive. In 1851 By
1851, the Gnomish Technocracy exists only in legend and ruins, its
disappearance leaving a void both literal and metaphorical. The
Reclaimer's League has taken up the mantle of uncovering the lost
nation's secrets, its expeditions sparking new debates and discoveries.
The Technocracy's legacy serves as both a cautionary tale and an
enduring source of fascination, a reminder of the perils of unchecked
ambition and the mysteries of the cosmos. Rim Exterior Colonies Nation
in early 3E In the early Third Era, the Rim Exterior Colonies did not
yet exist as a unified entity. Instead, they were scattered settlements
formed by refugees fleeing the Titan Exodus in crude, jury-rigged ships.
Life was brutal and unforgiving on the edge of the galaxy. Cut off from
the rest of civilization, early colonists faced starvation, infighting,
and resource depletion. Despite these hardships, the egalitarian
principles of survival through cooperation began to emerge. Colonies
relied on shared labor, communal resource management, and collective
decision-making to endure. By the end of this period, the idea of unity
across the rim began to take root, as leaders realized that their
survival depended on collaboration.

Nation in 851 By 851, the Rim Exterior Colonies had solidified into a
pragmatic and egalitarian society. Governed by the Egalitarian Assembly,
a council where every colony sent elected representatives, the Rim
balanced resource management, deep-space exploration, and mutual
defense. With their advanced Darklight Engines, they had mastered the
treacherous void and connected their far-flung systems into a cohesive
whole. Economically, the Rim relied on mining rogue asteroids, siphoning
dying stars for energy, and salvaging wreckage from deep space. Their
ingenuity in resource extraction and recycling gave them leverage as
skilled traders and frontier specialists. However, life on the edge of
the galaxy remained difficult. Starvation and technological failure were
constant threats, while rumors of cosmic anomalies and rogue experiments
kept colonists wary of the void. The Rim's growing stability and
resilience earned them recognition, but they remained cautious of
outside powers, their isolation shaping a culture of independence and
self-reliance. From 150 to 1851 Year 150 - the Exodus to the Rim As the
chaos of the Titan Exodus ravaged the Titancore, fleets of desperate
refugees, representing many races and cultures, fled further than anyone
thought possible. They reached the dying stars and barren systems at the
galactic rim, founding scattered colonies and outposts in the void.
These initial settlers relied on crude salvage, rationing, and brutal
determination to survive. Year 300 - the Starvation Years The Starvation
Years saw near-collapse across the rim colonies. Isolated by distance
and lacking resources, entire settlements vanished due to famine and
infighting. In response, survivors adopted a colony egalitarianism,
prioritizing shared resources, equitable labor, and council-led
governance. The First Rim Accord was signed, linking colonies into an
early cooperative network that ensured mutual survival. Year 550 - the
Founding of the Deepmines Guild The Deepmines Guild was formed as
engineers and miners devised innovative methods to harvest minerals from
rogue asteroids and the dying stars of the rim. This guild became a
cornerstone of the Rim economy, ensuring colonies had enough resources
to stabilize and expand. Star-mining rigs, crafted from scavenged
technology, became iconic symbols of Rim ingenuity. Year 1000 - the Rim
Charter Signed The Rim Charter formally unified the colonies into a
single nation-state under the Egalitarian Assembly, composed of elected
representatives from every colony world. The Charter enshrined equality,
shared prosperity, and deep-space independence as core ideals. This
milestone marked the transformation of the Rim from scattered
settlements to a unified, pragmatic nation. Year 1200 - the Darklight
Engines Perfected Rim engineers perfected Darklight Engines, a
propulsion breakthrough allowing safe and efficient travel between rim
systems. This technology, tailored to the long and treacherous journeys
at the galaxy's edge, connected isolated colonies and allowed
resource-gathering expeditions to reach further than ever before. Year
1400 - the Silent Collapse Several outer colonies experienced
catastrophic failures of life support systems, and communication was
mysteriously lost. Rumors of rogue void-technology experiments and
cosmic anomalies spread fear across the rim. The Egalitarian Assembly
intervened, consolidating vulnerable systems into larger, fortified hubs
and banning unregulated technological experimentation.

Year 1690 - Joining the Galactic League of States The Rim Exterior
Colonies joined the Galactic League of States before it's formal
establishment, presenting themselves as industrious frontier settlers
and pioneers of deep-space survival. They leveraged their resource
networks and navigation expertise to gain favor, though they remained
skeptical of central galactic governance and wary of foreign influence.
Year 1700 - Skirmish with Karak Ankor'dûm Forces A large-scale incursion
by Karak Ankor'dûm, the formidable Dwarven military power, triggered a
brutal confrontation. Rim fleets, unprepared for the might of Rune-Mechs
and the Skyforged Armada, suffered devastating losses. Though the
Dwarves pulled back without claiming territory, the event sparked
outrage within the Rim and across the Galactic League of States. The
Dwarves' withdrawal was perceived as disdainful, with no explanation
provided. The battle became a major controversy in the League, fueling
debates about frontier defense and mutual protection agreements. Year
1750 - the Rimward Beacon Project In response to growing threats and to
strengthen their defenses, the Rimward Beacon Project was launched.
Massive beacons---deep-space lighthouses powered by Darklight
Engines---were constructed at key systems along the rim, serving as
navigational aids and early-warning stations. These lighthouses
safeguarded trade, exploration, and colonization efforts while
protecting the rim's fragile independence. In 1851 By 1851, the Rim
Exterior Colonies had become a cornerstone of deep-space exploration and
frontier resilience. Their Egalitarian Assembly had matured into a
stable, if fractious, political body that prioritized shared prosperity
and equal representation. The Rimward Beacon Project, completed in 1750,
transformed the rim into a welldefended frontier, with powerful
navigational lighthouses guiding trade and exploration while serving as
bulwarks against incursions. The Skirmish of 1700 with Karak Ankor'dûm
remained a bitter memory, a reminder of the Rim's vulnerability to
larger galactic powers. Though no territory was lost, the scars of the
conflict lingered, and it fueled decades of technological advancement
and military preparation. The Rim's fleets, though small, became
renowned for their adaptability, outfitted with mining lasers and
Darklight-powered defenses that allowed them to punch above their
weight. Culturally, the Rim remained fiercely egalitarian, with a strong
emphasis on communal survival, technological ingenuity, and respect for
the void. While others viewed the galactic edge as barren and
inhospitable, the colonists saw it as a place of freedom and
opportunity---a frontier they had tamed through grit and unity. As
members of the Galactic League of States, the Rim Exterior Colonies
occupied a unique position: a resource-rich frontier state, a bastion of
independence, and a people who had made the void their home. Second
Elrossi Imperium Nation in early 3E At the dawn of the Third Era, the
Second Elrossi Imperium stood as a beacon of hope and unity for
humanity, guided by the unbroken von Sternstahl dynasty. Founded by the
paladin-king Emperor Maximilian von Sternstahl in 500 3E, the Imperium's
roots lay in the shattered remnants of humanity's colonies after Orrin's
Grip. The reincarnation of Maximilian was seen as a divine sign, as he
united scattered human worlds under the banner of Elross while
maintaining respect for the Yu Pantheon. The Imperium's early years were
defined by the First of the Third Crusade, a massive campaign to reclaim
the lost Myr Clusters. Though the crusade ultimately failed, it
established the Imperium as a formidable military power and a symbol of
divine order.

Nation in 851 By 851, the Second Elrossi Imperium was a thriving
theocratic empire, having consolidated control over vast regions of the
Inner Straits. The von Sternstahl dynasty remained unbroken, with
Emperor Frederick III ruling from the golden spires of Elyria, the heart
of the Imperium. The Imperium had become a cultural and military titan,
its armies bolstered by the elite Order of the Silver Lion and its trade
routes secured through diplomatic accords. However, internal divisions
simmered beneath the surface. The theological schism between the Orders
of the Silver Lion and Gilded Sun weakened the Imperium's unity, while
discontented noble houses sought to challenge the Emperor's centralized
authority. From 851 to 1851 Year 851 - The Aegis Concord The Imperium
establishes the Aegis Concord, an alliance of vassal states under the
rule of Emperor Ferdinand IV von Sternstahl. This treaty centralizes
military power, allowing the Imperium to consolidate fragmented
territories. Year 900 - The Scholar's Reformation The Priests of the
First Star publish the Celestial Codices, a theological doctrine that
merges the teachings of Elross with advanced astrological sciences. This
sparks a cultural renaissance and a surge in exploration. Year 950 - The
Twilight Purge Emperor Wilhelm III orders the Twilight Purge, a campaign
to root out heretical sects secretly worshiping Orrin. Entire noble
houses are excommunicated, leading to a wave of exiles and power shifts
within the nobility. Year 1010 - The Silver Crown Accords A rare moment
of peace is achieved as the Silver Crown Accords establish formal
borders between the Imperium and neighboring nations. Trade flourishes,
and cultural exchange with the Dwarven Clansystems begins. Year 1085 -
The Starlight Schism Religious tension escalates when dissenters within
the Church of Elross challenge the Imperium's authority, leading to the
Starlight Schism. The dissenters establish the Order of the Radiant
Dawn, leading to decades of civil unrest. Year 1120 - The Imperial
Reunification Under Emperor Leopold II, the schism is resolved through
diplomacy and military might. The Radiant Dawn is disbanded, and their
leaders are offered amnesty in exchange for loyalty. Year 1200 - The
Oration of Elyria Empress Valeria von Sternstahl delivers the Oration of
Elyria, reaffirming the Imperium's divine mandate and introducing
reforms that empower noble houses while restricting commoners'
influence. This entrenches the aristocracy's dominance. Year 1265 - The
Myr Incident In an attempt to expand influence into the Myr Clusters,
the Imperium offered support to an independent Princedom. After
centuries of supporting him and his kin, the Imperium suffers a
humiliating defeat at the hands of a coalition of warlords and
mercenaries - although not directly allied to eachother - it sparked a
reevaluation of military and diplomatic strategies.

Year 1300 - The Excommunicated Houses Several noble houses, dissatisfied
with the centralized power of the Emperor, declare independence,
claiming a dozen sectors. The rebellion plunges the Imperium into a
15-year civil war, ending in a stalemate. The rebelling houses are
excommunicated after their death and blood magics are discovered, but
allowed to govern independently as rogue states due to the overwhelming
cost of invading these sectors. Year 1320 - The Celestial Tribunal A
reformist emperor, Maximilian II, establishes the Celestial Tribunal, a
body of scholars, priests, and generals tasked with managing crises and
guiding imperial policy. This cements the Imperium's bureaucracy. Year
1420 - The Succession War of Shadows A contested imperial succession
results in the Succession War of Shadows, where factions within the
nobility clash for control. The war ends with the ascension of Emperor
Aldric the Stern, known for his ruthless efficiency. Year 1520 - The
Mechanist Reforms Inspired by Dwarven engineering, the Mechanist Reforms
modernize the Imperium's military, introducing hybridized technologies
salvaged from the Wastes. The reforms transform the Imperium into a
technological powerhouse. Year 1640 - The Starfall Campaigns The
Imperium launches the Starfall Campaigns, a series of expeditions into
unclaimed systems. While moderately successful, they strain resources,
spark dissent among the vassal states, and the eyes of the galactic
community as they worry for the smaller planetary societies neighbouring
the Imperium. Year 1720 - The Redemption Crusade The Imperium launches a
crusade against the Rusted Legion, accusing them of heresy and
desecration of Elrossi relics. The war ends inconclusively, but it
further entrenches the Imperium's role as a defender of faith. During
this crusade, the Emperor manages to bring the Second Elrossi Imperium
into the Galactic League of States, although many nobles protest this
decision. Year 1700 - Tendrils of Decay The necrotic armies of Orro
launch a devastating invasion of the Second Elrossi Imperium, unleashing
the infamous Tendrils of Decay across several sectors. Gravitational
necrotic magic drags massive Bone Worlds into Elrossi systems,
annihilating fleets and colonies in their path. Though the invasion
grinds to a halt after two decades of brutal resistance, the scars of
war linger to the modern day, with holy Elrossi leaders proclaiming it
would take centuries to cleanse every system of Orro's lingering
corruption. Year 1851 - A Divided Unity The Imperium stands as a beacon
of faith and power, yet internal tensions between reformists and
traditionalists threaten its unity. Its inclusion in the Galactic League
of States solidifies its role as a galactic power, though at the cost of
greater scrutiny and accountability. Internally, the Imperium focuses on
re-establishing control over regions lost to the Excommunicated Houses,
the Tendrils of Decay, and the sectors lost to the Avatar of Entropy
centuries prior. In 1851 By 1851, the Second Elrossi Imperium had
solidified its position as a galactic superpower and a central member of
the Galactic League of States. Emperor Lucas von Sternstahl, a cunning
diplomat and devout ruler, presided over a nation that had transitioned
from expansionist crusades to a focus on internal stability and external
diplomacy.

The Imperium's strength lay in its blend of faith and military might.
The hundreds of holy orders and noble houses ensured the security of its
borders, while its cathedrals and academies produced some of the
galaxy's most renowned scholars and clerics. The cultural renaissance
initiated in the 17th century continued to enrich the Imperium,
spreading its influence through art, literature, and religious devotion.
However, challenges remained. The scars of the Tendrils of Decay and the
inconclusive Redemption Crusade had drained the Imperium's resources,
and the secessionist noble houses from the Great Secession continued to
act as a thorn in its side. Meanwhile, tensions with the Rusted Legion
and other rival nations persisted, threatening the Imperium's hard-won
peace. Despite these challenges, the Imperium's faith in its divine
mandate endured. The von Sternstahl dynasty, with its lineage traced
back to the holy twin brothers of the Second Era, remained a symbol of
unbroken continuity and divine favor. Under Emperor Lucas's leadership,
the Second Elrossi Imperium faced the future with a mix of caution and
confidence, determined to uphold its sacred mission as a light in the
galaxy's darkness. Silkstrider Realm Nation in early 3E In the early
years of the Third Era, the Drider of the Silkstrider Realm emerged as a
rising power in the Webbed Systems, an isolated and resource-rich region
of the galaxy's southwestern rim. Guided by their enigmatic High
Priestess, a figure believed to commune directly with their goddess from
the Orrinic Pantheon, the Drider unified their scattered web-like
colonies into a cohesive civilization. Their mastery of time magic,
taught through ancient rituals and said to be gifted by their goddess,
became a cornerstone of their society. This arcane prowess was reflected
in their Timeweaver Archers, elite warriors capable of bending time to
slow their enemies or hasten their own strikes, making them formidable
in skirmishes. Despite their technological and magical advancements, the
Drider remained fiercely isolationist, avoiding galactic politics while
sending out swift, surgical raiding parties to capture resources and
slaves from unaligned worlds. Their starships, sleek and web-like in
design, navigated the treacherous starfields of the Webbed Systems with
unmatched precision, creating an impregnable domain that marked them as
both a mystery and a menace to the galaxy. Nation in 851 By 851 AG, the
Silkstrider Realm had expanded into a sprawling yet insular dominion
over the Webbed Systems, commanding respect and fear across the galactic
rim. The High Priestess Aelthara Venomspire, known as the "Weaver of
Fates," ruled with divine authority, her decisions shaping every aspect
of Drider life. The Drider maintained their strict isolationist stance,
refusing entry to outsiders and dealing harshly with intruders who
ventured too close to their web-like borders. Their time magic, now
woven into every facet of their society, allowed them to predict and
counter potential threats with uncanny accuracy, further solidifying
their unassailable position. The Timeweaver Archers, enhanced by
centuries of refinement in their techniques, were now legendary figures,
often the subject of fearful tales told by those who survived Drider
raids. These raids, conducted with surgical precision, ensured a steady
flow of slaves and resources into the Realm, but their scope was limited
to preserve the Drider's isolation. The Eternal Loom, a massive
temple-complex dedicated to their goddess and Orrin, became the
spiritual heart of the Silkstrider Realm, a place where time itself
seemed to flow differently. While their society thrived in
self-sufficiency and arcane mastery, whispers of dissent stirred among
the slave populations and a few ambitious Drider lords who questioned
the High Priestess's unwavering adherence to isolationism. By 851 AG,
the Silkstrider Realm was a nation shrouded in mystery and power, its
isolation both a strength and a vulnerability, as it navigated the
galactic age on its own enigmatic terms. From 851 to 1851 860 - The Loom
Wars Begin

The Silkstrider Realm's conflict with the Old Horde, an expansive and
militaristic neighboring power, escalates into a prolonged war. Drider
Timeweaver Archers and their magically enhanced raiding ships engage the
brutish armies of the Old Horde, which rely on overwhelming numbers and
relentless assaults. 900 - The Siege of Venomspire Citadel The Old Horde
launches a massive assault on the Venomspire Citadel, one of the Realm's
oldest and most fortified colonies. The Drider repel the attack through
a desperate gambit led by the High Priestess, who manipulates time magic
to accelerate the decay of the Horde's war engines, turning the tide of
battle. This marks a turning point in the war, demonstrating the
unmatched precision of Drider tactics. 1000 - The Collapse of the Old
Horde The death of the last grandson of the Warchief results in the
disintegration of the Old Horde into smaller factions, including the
Ironfang Dominion and the Bloodscale Kin. The war officially ends, and
the Silkstrider Realm, though victorious, turns inward to recover from
the century of conflict. The Eternal Loom, the spiritual center of the
Drider, is consecrated to honor the victory and those lost in the war.
1100 - The Scouring of the Webbed Frontier With the Old Horde shattered,
the Silkstrider Realm turns its attention to the uncivilized systems
around its borders. Raiding parties eliminate scattered groups of Ikzo
Lizardmen and Azezz Gnollpacks, securing resources and expanding the
Realm's influence deeper into the Webbed Systems. However, their raids
provoke intermittent retaliations from factions like the Nikios Khanate
of Centaur. 1250 - The Temporal Accord The Drider develop new
advancements in time magic through the Temporal Accord, a decree by the
High Priestess that organizes the most gifted Timeweavers into research
conclaves. This leads to breakthroughs in Timestream Manipulation,
allowing the Drider to accelerate the growth of crops, fortify
structures, and create defensive temporal fields around their cities.
1320 - The Rise of the Widow's Web Guilds The Widow's Web, a network of
elite mercenary guilds within the Realm, grows in prominence, offering
their services as raiders and assassins to nearby factions for
exorbitant fees. This practice skirts the Realm's isolationist policies
but provides a steady influx of wealth and intelligence from outside
powers. 1400 - The Silent Intrusion A mysterious incursion by an unknown
faction---later referred to as the Phantom Raiders---targets several
outposts on the borders of the Silkstrider Realm. The Drider's Order of
the Coiled Fang is tasked with defending the territory and manages to
repel the threat, though the identity of the attackers remains unknown.
This event prompts heightened patrols and tighter security across the
Webbed Systems. 1525 - The Loomkeeper's Schism A philosophical divide
within the Temporal Conclave erupts into the Loomkeeper's Schism, as
radical Timeweavers argue for the abandonment of temporal restrictions
in pursuit of unlimited power. The High Priestess suppresses the
rebellion, but the damage to the Conclave delays advancements in time
magic for nearly a century. 1650 - The Conquest of the Webbed Void The
Silkstrider Realm reaches its zenith in territorial expansion as the
High Priestess orders the conquest of the Webbed Void, a series of
resource-rich systems beyond the rim. This marks a turning point as the
Drider push the limits of their isolationist stance, incorporating new
resources while maintaining strict control over the flow of outsiders.
1750 - The Green Accord with the Jade Pact

The Silkstrider Realm establishes a tentative truce with the neighboring
Jade Pact, allowing limited trade and cooperation while maintaining
strict border control. The Green Accord fosters a cautious but mutually
beneficial relationship, with the Pact providing agricultural goods in
exchange for advanced magical tools crafted by the Drider. 1820 - The
Eternal Weave Initiative The High Priestess launches the Eternal Weave
Initiative, a monumental project aimed at consolidating the Realm's
holdings by constructing massive temporal stabilizers across the Webbed
Systems. These devices not only protect the Realm from temporal
anomalies but also enhance their ability to manipulate time on a large
scale, reinforcing their dominance in the region. 1851 - A Timeless
Dominion By 1851, the Silkstrider Realm is a powerful and enigmatic
intergalactic nation, its borders tightly controlled and its
isolationist policies unwavering. The advancements from the Eternal
Weave Initiative have solidified their mastery of time magic, making
them unparalleled in their manipulation of the timestream. While their
raids have decreased in frequency, their influence over the Webbed
Systems remains absolute, their dominion a testament to the relentless
ambition and arcane mastery of the Drider. In 1851 By 1851 AG, the
Silkstrider Realm has fully established itself as a mysterious and
highly advanced power, dominating the Webbed Systems through a
combination of isolationism, time magic, and precise military
operations. The High Priestess rules with divine authority, her decrees
shaping the Realm's expansion and its careful navigation of galactic
politics. While their technological and magical advancements make them a
formidable power, the Drider's strict isolationism ensures they remain
enigmatic, their borders closed to outsiders save for a select few
diplomatic agreements. Their raids are now rare but devastating,
maintaining their reputation as both feared predators and guardians of
their timeless domain. Slate Syndicate Rise of a Nation In the aftermath
of the Concrete Collapse, what was once a sprawling criminal empire lay
in tatters. Goblin warlords, scrap lords, and smugglers tore into the
wreckage, each seeking to claim what was left. From this chaos, a group
of the most ruthless and cunning survivors formed the Broken Ledger,
uniting under the banner of the Slate Syndicate. The Syndicate was no
nation---it was a web of feuding factions held together by necessity and
greed. Goblins formed the backbone, salvaging what tech and
infrastructure they could while fostering a culture of cutthroat
ingenuity. Warships were cobbled together from wreckage, and entire
economies relied on black-market dealings. For the first century, the
Slate Syndicate stumbled and bled, surviving only because no one
believed them worth the trouble to crush. Founding to 1851 1400 - Birth
of the Slate Syndicate After the Concrete Collapse, surviving goblin
warlords scramble to seize power over the wreckage. A council calling
itself the Broken Ledger is formed, claiming the title of the Slate
Syndicate, a play on "wiping the slate clean" of their former failures.
1450 - The Scrappers' Concord

The Slate Syndicate formally integrates hundreds of goblin salvage clans
that roam the ruins of Concrete Cartel space. These clans provide
critical materials and tech to the Syndicate, but they are fiercely
independent and hard to control. 1510 - Black Chains Revolt A group of
human and ratfolk slave populations rises up against the Syndicate.
Though the rebellion is crushed, the revolt weakens the Syndicate's
economy and destabilizes several systems, leading to decades of
infighting among warlords. 1600 - The Nexus Smuggling Pact Facing
galactic isolation, the Slate Syndicate brokers a hidden pact with New
Xerxes and various black-market players in Blackstar Corp to serve as
middlemen for illegal technology shipments. This strengthens their
foothold in the underworld but escalates conflict with neighboring
governments. 1700 - The Rust Purge Fearing growing unrest among the
salvage clans, the Broken Ledger enforces a brutal purge of rebellious
leaders. Thousands die, and entire salvage fleets are dismantled. While
it restores temporary order, it causes a lasting rift between the
warlords and the goblin clans. 1805 - Raid on Belinore Shipping Lanes In
a bold move, Slate Syndicate fleets raid Belinore's shipping lanes,
seizing advanced navigational technology. The raid is condemned by the
Galactic League and sparks brief skirmishes with Belinore's forces.
1851 - The Syndicate Holds By 1851, the Slate Syndicate clings to
survival. Despite external pressures, it remains a critical player in
the galactic black market, feeding the greed of legitimate governments
and corporations that prefer to keep their dealings in the shadows. In
1851 The Slate Syndicate in 1851 is a paradox: a nation held together by
chaos and greed. The Broken Ledger continues to rule, though their
authority is tenuous at best. Goblin salvage fleets dominate the
Syndicate's economy, scavenging derelict stations and trading scrap for
weapons and rare metals. Rogue factions of humans and ratfolk serve as
mercenaries and spies, spreading the Syndicate's influence through
illicit dealings. Despite repeated attempts to crush them, the Syndicate
thrives in the galaxy's shadow. Their recent raid on Belinore proves
their boldness, but it also brings increased scrutiny. The Galactic
League remains united in denying the Syndicate legitimacy, though many
of its members secretly rely on Syndicate smugglers to move illegal
goods. For all their setbacks, the Slate Syndicate persists. They are
the dark underbelly of the galaxy---a broken empire that refuses to die,
thriving amidst rust and ruin. Sovranty of Sin'dal Nation in early 3E
The Sovranty of Sin'dal begins as an extension of Gro'May's divine
empire, established to serve as a beacon of elven culture and magic.
Under the guidance of Caledor Sin'dal, the Sovranty becomes a place of
arcane mastery, where homunculi and kin occupy key roles in governance.
Though initially a harmonious vassal, the Sovranty begins to assert its
identity as Caledor's ambitions grow.

Nation in 851 By 851 AG, the Sovranty of Sin'dal is a thriving elven
realm within the Cohorts of Thae'Viriel. Its economy is driven by guilds
specializing in celestial tools and mana redistribution, while its
culture is steeped in devotion to Gro'May. Caledor's homunculi govern
with precision, blending faith and efficiency to maintain order. The
Sovranty is celebrated as a model vassal state, though whispers of
Caledor's ambitions linger. From 851 to 1851 Year 851 - The Hierophant's
Directive Caledor Sin'dal begins a campaign to consolidate the
Sovranty's governance. His homunculi assume critical administrative
roles, solidifying his rule while allowing kin to occupy ceremonial
positions. This balance strengthens central authority without alienating
the traditional nobility. Year 890 - The Astral Mirror Concord The
Sovranty establishes a cultural exchange with the Q'Xorlarrin Domain,
focusing on arcane innovation. This agreement results in shared
advancements in void magic and mana-weaving but also fosters covert
